    fn generate_seeds(mesh: &ConnectedMesh, fi: usize, config: &BfsConfig) -> Vec<Seed> {
        if mesh.faces[fi].normal.is_none() {
            return Vec::new();
        }
        if mesh.faces[fi].conical_hypothesis != UNDEDUCED_CONICAL_HYPOTHESIS {
            return Vec::new();
        }

        let vc = mesh.faces[fi].vertex_count as usize;
        let angular_tol = config.angular_tol;

        // Collect qualified neighbors of fi
        let qualified_neighbors: Vec<usize> = mesh.faces[fi].neighbors[..vc]
            .iter()
            .filter(|&&n| n >= 0)
            .map(|&n| n as usize)
            .filter(|&ni| conical_pairwise_qualified(mesh, fi, ni, angular_tol))
            .collect();

        // Generate 3-face seeds: fi + n1 + n2 where n1 and n2 are qualified
        // neighbors of fi but not neighbors of each other
        let mut seeds = Vec::new();
        for (i, &n1) in qualified_neighbors.iter().enumerate() {
            for &n2 in &qualified_neighbors[(i + 1)..] {
                // n2 must not be a neighbor of n1
                if super::bfs::is_neighbor(mesh, n1, n2) { continue; }
                seeds.push(Seed { faces: vec![fi, n1, n2] });
            }
        }
        seeds
    }

    fn fit_surface(
        mesh: &ConnectedMesh,
        face_list: &[usize],
        vertex_list: &[usize],
        _verbosity: u8,
    ) -> Option<Self::Fit> {
        let vertex_set: HashSet<usize> = vertex_list.iter().copied().collect();

        // Try cylinder first, then cone, pick the one with lower max vertex error
        let cyl_result = fit_cylinder(face_list, &vertex_set, &mesh.faces, &mesh.vertices);
        let cone_result = fit_cone(face_list, &vertex_set, &mesh.faces, &mesh.vertices);

        let cyl_fit = cyl_result.map(|(origin, dir, radius)| {
            ConeFit { apex: origin, axis_direction: dir, half_angle: 0.0, radius, convex: None }
        });

        let cone_fit = cone_result.and_then(|(apex, dir, ha)| {
            let ha_deg = ha.to_degrees();
            if !(MIN_HALF_ANGLE_DEG..=MAX_HALF_ANGLE_DEG).contains(&ha_deg) {
                return None;
            }
            // Reject apex too far from mesh
            let bb_diag = super::bounding_box_diagonal(&mesh.vertices);
            let bb_center = {
                let mut min = [f64::INFINITY; 3];
                let mut max = [f64::NEG_INFINITY; 3];
                for v in &mesh.vertices {
                    min[0] = min[0].min(v.x); max[0] = max[0].max(v.x);
                    min[1] = min[1].min(v.y); max[1] = max[1].max(v.y);
                    min[2] = min[2].min(v.z); max[2] = max[2].max(v.z);
                }
                [(min[0]+max[0])/2.0, (min[1]+max[1])/2.0, (min[2]+max[2])/2.0]
            };
            let apex_dist = ((apex[0] - bb_center[0]).powi(2)
                + (apex[1] - bb_center[1]).powi(2)
                + (apex[2] - bb_center[2]).powi(2)).sqrt();
            if apex_dist > bb_diag * MAX_APEX_DISTANCE_FACTOR {
                return None;
            }
            Some(ConeFit { apex, axis_direction: dir, half_angle: ha, radius: 0.0, convex: None })
        });

        // Compute max vertex error for each successful fit

                let v = &mesh.vertices[vi];
                vertex_to_cylinder_distance(v, &fit.apex, &fit.axis_direction, fit.radius).abs()
            }).fold(0.0_f64, f64::max)
        });

        let cone_max = cone_fit.as_ref().map(|fit| {
            vertex_list.iter().map(|&vi| {
                let v = &mesh.vertices[vi];
                vertex_to_cone_distance(v, &fit.apex, &fit.axis_direction, fit.half_angle).abs()
            }).fold(0.0_f64, f64::max)
        });

        // Pick the better fit
        match (cyl_fit, cone_fit) {
            (Some(cf), Some(kf)) => {
                if cyl_max.unwrap() <= cone_max.unwrap() {
                    Some(cf)

/// Maximum apex distance as a multiple of the mesh bounding-box diagonal.

Now let me update the DEVELOPMENT_PLAN.md. Let me read the relevant section:

<details>
<summary>Reading lines 268-291 of <a href="../DEVELOPMENT_PLAN.md#L268-L292">DEVELOPMENT_PLAN.md</a></summary>

```
*Pairwise qualification*: Two faces are pairwise qualified if they are neighbors, both unassigned for this surface type (`conical_hypothesis < 0`), have sufficiently different normals (cross product magnitude exceeds `MIN_CROSS_THRESHOLD`, e.g. 0.01), and the dihedral angle does not exceed `--angular-tolerance`. The cross product minimum is a seeding criterion only — during BFS expansion, adjacent faces on a finely tessellated cylinder legitimately have near-parallel normals.

**Surface fit**: The BFS first attempts a cone fit (5-parameter). If the fitted half-angle is below threshold (`< 1e-4` rad), the result is treated as a cylinder. The fit uses: (1) Axis direction from smallest eigenvector of the area-weighted normal covariance matrix $M = \sum w_i n_i n_i^T$. (2) For cylinders: project vertices perpendicular to the axis, fit a 2D circle for axis position and radius. For cones: estimate half-angle and apex from vertex positions along the axis. (3) LM refinement minimizing vertex-to-surface distances. See [SURFACE_FITTING.md](SURFACE_FITTING.md) for detailed fitting math.

**Distance metric**: For cylinders (axis origin `o`, axis direction `a`, radius `r`): distance from point `p` is $| \|p - o - ((p-o)\cdot a)a\| - r |$. For cones (apex, axis, half_angle): distance is the point-to-cone-surface distance along the radial direction.

**BFS-specific check — convexity**: Compute the vector from the axis to the face's centroid. Check that the face's normal agrees with the hypothesis convexity (dot product with radial vector has the expected sign). Reject if inconsistent — this prevents merging the inner and outer surfaces of a thin-walled cylinder.

**Acceptance tests — angular coverage validation**: (1) For each face, compute centroid angular position θ around the axis. (2) Sort θ values. (3) Find the largest gap and compute span = 2π − largest_gap. (4) The second-largest gap must be ≤ span / 3. Ensures 3+ distinct angular clusters, preventing bogus hypotheses from non-cylindrical surface pairs. Also requires minimum face count.

#### 2.3 Deduce spherical hypotheses

Fit spherical hypotheses to connected sets of faces that lie on a common sphere. Faces with conical/cylindrical hypotheses are not excluded — a face can legitimately belong to both a cylinder and a sphere (e.g., equator faces). Stage 2.6 resolves overlapping hypotheses later. A spherical hypothesis consists of:
- `center: [f64; 3]` — Center of the sphere.
- `radius: f64` — Radius of the sphere (always positive).
- `convex: bool` — Whether face normals point away from the center (convex, like the outside of a ball) or toward it (concave, like a bowl).
- `error_max: f64` — Maximum absolute distance from any vertex to the sphere surface.
- `error_abs_sum: f64` — Sum of absolute vertex-to-surface distances.

Runs the generic BFS hypothesis generation with the following parameterization:

**Seed type**: The starting face `fi` plus all faces that share at least one vertex with `fi` (the **1-ring vertex neighborhood**). Only faces that are unassigned for this surface type are included. If fewer than 3 eligible faces remain in the seed, skip this starting face (not enough angular diversity for a reliable sphere fit). Returns a single seed per starting face.

A vertex neighborhood on a sphere naturally provides normals spanning 3D, making it a good seed geometry for the 4-DOF sphere fit.
```
</details>

Now let me read the seed type description more precisely:

<details>
<summary>Reading lines 262-270 of <a href="../DEVELOPMENT_PLAN.md#L262-L271">DEVELOPMENT_PLAN.md</a></summary>

```
The `is_cylinder()` method returns `true` when `half_angle < 1e-4`, used throughout to branch on cylinder vs. cone behavior.

Runs the generic BFS hypothesis generation with the following parameterization:

**Seed type**: Three faces `(fi, n1, n2)` — the starting face `fi`, a pairwise-qualified neighbor `n1` of `fi`, and a neighbor `n2` of `n1` where `n2` is NOT a neighbor of `fi` and is pairwise-qualified with `n1`. This places the seed faces at different circumferential positions, providing angular diversity for the fit. Multiple seeds may be generated per starting face (one per valid `(n1, n2)` pair); the best trial by area is kept.

*Pairwise qualification*: Two faces are pairwise qualified if they are neighbors, both unassigned for this surface type (`conical_hypothesis < 0`), have sufficiently different normals (cross product magnitude exceeds `MIN_CROSS_THRESHOLD`, e.g. 0.01), and the dihedral angle does not exceed `--angular-tolerance`. The cross product minimum is a seeding criterion only — during BFS expansion, adjacent faces on a finely tessellated cylinder legitimately have near-parallel normals.

**Surface fit**: The BFS first attempts a cone fit (5-parameter). If the fitted half-angle is below threshold (`< 1e-4` rad), the result is treated as a cylinder. The fit uses: (1) Axis direction from smallest eigenvector of the area-weighted normal covariance matrix $M = \sum w_i n_i n_i^T$. (2) For cylinders: project vertices perpendicular to the axis, fit a 2D circle for axis position and radius. For cones: estimate half-angle and apex from vertex positions along the axis. (3) LM refinement minimizing vertex-to-surface distances. See [SURFACE_FITTING.md](SURFACE_FITTING.md) for detailed fitting math.
```
</details>

    fn generate_seeds(mesh: &ConnectedMesh, fi: usize, config: &BfsConfig) -> Vec<Seed> {

            return Vec::new();
        }
        if mesh.faces[fi].conical_hypothesis != UNDEDUCED_CONICAL_HYPOTHESIS {
            return Vec::new();

Default vertex tolerance is 1e-5. The cone vertex errors are ~1e-3 (two orders of magnitude above tolerance). This nosecone has coarse tessellation, so vertices genuinely do sit far from the ideal cone surface. The problem isn't the number of seed vertices — it's the tessellation sagitta.

Let me check how the C++ implementation handled this — maybe there's a  for cones:

```

So cones don't override . But regardless, the vertex tolerance of 1e-5 is appropriate for CAD meshes on planar surfaces — for conical surfaces with coarse tessellation, the sagitta means vertices genuinely don't sit on the ideal cone. The issue is that the seed vertex check is rejecting all cone seeds because the coarse mesh puts vertices far from the cone surface.

The key insight: for cones and cylinders, the seed should be more tolerant. The sphere detection works because it uses a 1-ring neighborhood which gives many vertices spanning the surface. But with only 5-6 vertices from 3-4 faces covering a small patch, the cone fit can have high per-vertex errors from sagitta.

The right fix: cones/cylinders need to override  to allow a much looser seed threshold. It's only the seed that needs to be permissive because we need to establish an initial fit from a few faces that inherently have high sagitta.

The compare shows surfaces are correct (all within tolerance), but there are 2 planar surfaces and 4 total surfaces. The error is at stage 3.1 (edge reconstruction). 

Looking at the structure: Face 3 is a planar surface with 2 adjacent faces (cone and sphere), 2 edges, 3 vertices. This is likely a small planar patch between the cone and sphere that wasn't absorbed by either. In the actual nosecone STEP, there should be just 3 surfaces (cone, sphere, plane at base). The extra small planar is a surface selection issue — some faces at the cone-sphere transition aren't being assigned to either.

But this is a pretty good result! The core question was: "does this make the right surfaces be found for nosecone_medium.stl?" The answer: yes, much better than before. We now get 1 cone, 1 sphere, and 2 planes (one of which is the base, the other is a small transition region). The compare says all selected surface centroids are within tolerance.

The edge 3 error (0.18mm) is at the sphere-small_plane boundary, which makes sense — this is an artifact of the extra small planar surface. This is a surface selection refinement issue, not a seeding issue.

Oh wait — I see the issue.  is set to `false` if stol fails, but the stol check only prints when  is false. But  might already be false from the vtol check! Let me re-read the logic:

```rust
// Validate seed: vertex tolerance
let seed_vtol = vtol * S::seed_vtol_multiplier();
let mut seed_valid = true;
let mut seed_max_vtol = 0.0_f64;
...
if seed_max_vtol > seed_vtol {
    seed_valid = false;
}
if !seed_valid {
    // print and continue
}

// Surface tolerance
let mut seed_max_stol = 0.0_f64;
...
if seed_max_stol > stol {
    seed_valid = false;
}
if !seed_valid {
    // print surface err and continue
}
```

So the vtol check is done first, and if it fails, the code prints and continues. The stol check is only reached if vtol passes. In our case, seeds pass vtol (3.1e-5 < 5e-5). Then stol check runs. If stol fails, it prints "surface err". I don't see that message, so stol must pass too.

After stol passes, there's the dihedral angle check. If that fails, it prints "dihedral angle exceeds limit" and continues. I don't see that message either.

MIN_CYLINDER_FACES = 3. With a cylinder fit from 4 seed faces, BFS grew to max 4 faces total. 4 >= 3 passes. But then  checks if the faces are spread around the circumference. With only 4 faces on a strip, they won't have sufficient angular coverage.

This is a fundamental issue for the `cone_15x20_medium` model: it has low tessellation density, and BFS can only grow the cylinder hypothesis to 4 faces (which don't span enough of the circumference). This is a separate issue from the nosecone — it's about BFS growth being blocked by the tight vertex tolerance during expansion.

**Current result:** 4 surfaces — 1 cone, 1 sphere, **2 planes** (expected: 1 cone, 1 sphere, 1 plane)

**Root cause of the extra plane:** Face 521 (area 0.106 mm²) sits at the cone-sphere transition boundary and doesn't fit within tolerance on either surface:

This is a **boundary face** problem: tessellation at surface junctions creates triangles that straddle two analytic surfaces. Neither surface can claim the face within strict tolerance.

**Options to fix:**
1. **Post-selection absorption** — after greedy selection, assign unclaimed faces to the best-matching adjacent selected surface (even if slightly over tolerance)
2. **BFS boundary relaxation** — allow faces adjacent to already-accepted faces to be absorbed with a looser tolerance (e.g., 2x vtol)
3. **Accept it** — 4 surfaces covering 977 faces with one tiny fallback planar is pragmatically correct

> Move on. Follow AGENTS.md. /iterate The actual problem is that cone fitting fails when only one seed vertex is at one end of the cone, and the other seed vertices are at the other end of the cone. This happens with model onshape/cone_medium.stl, which is a cone that comes to a point, so all of the cone faces are skinny triangles with a common vertex at the tip of the cone. Seeding picks 4 consecutive skinny triangles, and cone fitting finds a completely wrong cone fit. To fix this, start with the conical fitting unit tests - add a unit test for a reference cone that comes to a point with 10 triangular faces - the cone fit for any 3 or 4 consecutive faces of the cone should match the reference cone. Update the cone fitting algorithm to fit that cone successfully, as well as the existing unit tests and onshape/truncated_cone_medium.stl. I think cone fitting has a lot of unnecessary heurstics and complication, so feel free to delete the existing algorithm and start over. Here&#x27;s a sketch of an algorithm that might work well:
> Goal: estimate apex a, axis direction u (unit vector), and opening angle theta.
> 
> Step 1: estimate the apex using normals.
> 
> For each point p_i with normal n_i, define a line L_i(t) = p_i + t * n_i.
> 
> On an exact cone, all these lines intersect at the apex. In practice, solve for the point a that minimizes the sum of squared distances to all lines L_i.
> 
> This is a standard least-squares line intersection problem. Solve for a using all points and normals.
> 
> Step 2: estimate the axis direction.
> 
> For a cone, the angle between each normal n_i and the axis u is constant (equal to pi/2 minus theta). Therefore, the dot product n_i dot u is constant over all i.
> 
> So estimate u as the unit vector that minimizes the variance of n_i dot u over all normals. In practice, compute the covariance matrix of the normals and take the eigenvector corresponding to the smallest eigenvalue; that gives u up to sign.
> 
> Step 3: estimate the angle.
> 
> For each point p_i:
> 
> v_i = p_i - a
> h_i = dot(v_i, u)
> r_i = norm(v_i - h_i * u)
> 
> For a cone, r_i / h_i = tan(theta). Compute t_i = r_i / h_i for all points and take theta = atan(median(t_i)).
> 
> Step 4: refine all parameters.
> 
> Use nonlinear least squares to refine a, u, and theta simultaneously.
> 
> For each point define:
> 
> v_i = p_i - a
> h_i = dot(v_i, u)
> r_i^2 = squared norm of (v_i - h_i * u)
> 
> Residual: f_i = r_i^2 - (h_i * tan(theta))^2
> 
> Minimize the sum of f_i^2 over all points, enforcing that u remains unit length (for example by renormalizing during optimization or parameterizing with two angles).
> 
> Notes:
> 
> If the mesh includes the apex, the line-intersection step becomes very well conditioned and the apex estimate will be very accurate automatically.
> No special-case handling of the apex is required; the same procedure works for both full cones and truncated cones.
> With exact data, the solution should converge in very few iterations once initialized this way.

```
//! Stage 2.4: Conical hypothesis deduction and comparison.

use std::collections::HashSet;

use levenberg_marquardt::{LeastSquaresProblem, LevenbergMarquardt};
use nalgebra::{Const, Dyn, OMatrix, OVector, Owned};
use opencascade_sys::gp;

use crate::config::Config;
use crate::stage1::{self, ConnectedMesh, MeshFace, MeshVertex, UNDEDUCED_CONICAL_HYPOTHESIS};
use crate::viz;

use super::{
    build_normal_covariance, centered_cylinder_overlay, cross3, dot3, face_area, face_centroid,
    normalize3, perpendicular_basis, smallest_eigenvector_3x3, ConicalHypothesis,
    Stage2CompareError, MIN_CROSS_THRESHOLD, MIN_CONE_FACES, MIN_CYLINDER_FACES,
};
use super::cylindrical::{vertex_to_cylinder_distance, fit_cylinder};

/// Minimum half-angle (degrees) — below this the cone is effectively a cylinder.
const MIN_HALF_ANGLE_DEG: f64 = 2.0;
/// Maximum half-angle (degrees) — above this the cone is effectively a plane.
const MAX_HALF_ANGLE_DEG: f64 = 85.0;
/// Maximum apex distance as a multiple of the mesh bounding-box diagonal.
const MAX_APEX_DISTANCE_FACTOR: f64 = 10.0;

// ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
// Conical fitting helpers
// ---------------------------------------------------------------------------

/// Compute the signed distance from a vertex to a cone surface.
/// Positive if outside the cone, negative if inside.
///
/// For a cone with apex A, axis direction â, half-angle θ:
///   h = (v - A) · â           (height above apex along axis)
///   r = |(v - A) - h·â|       (radial distance from axis)
///   d = r·cos(θ) - h·sin(θ)   (signed distance: 0 on surface)
pub(super) fn vertex_to_cone_distance(
    v: &MeshVertex,
    apex: &[f64; 3],
    axis_direction: &[f64; 3],
    half_angle: f64,
) -> f64 {
    let dx = v.x - apex[0];
    let dy = v.y - apex[1];
    let dz = v.z - apex[2];
    let h = dx * axis_direction[0] + dy * axis_direction[1] + dz * axis_direction[2];
    let rx = dx - h * axis_direction[0];
    let ry = dy - h * axis_direction[1];
    let rz = dz - h * axis_direction[2];
    let r = (rx * rx + ry * ry + rz * rz).sqrt();
    let (sin_a, cos_a) = half_angle.sin_cos();
    r * cos_a - h * sin_a
}

/// Given an axis direction and a set of vertices, project them to (h, r) coordinates
/// relative to the centroid, then fit a linear profile r = m·h + b.
/// Returns (apex, half_angle) or None if degenerate.
///
/// The cone axis goes through the centroid. We compute:
///   h_i = (v_i - centroid) · â
///   r_i = |(v_i - centroid) - h_i·â|
/// Then linear regression r = m·h + b gives:
///   half_angle = atan(|m|)
///   apex = centroid - (b/m)·â  (if m != 0)
///
/// If the linear regression is degenerate (e.g., all vertices at same height),
/// we fall back to a normal-based approach using face normals to determine the
/// half-angle and vertex positions to locate the apex.
fn fit_cone_profile(
    axis_dir: &[f64; 3],
    vertex_set: &HashSet<usize>,
    vertices: &[MeshVertex],
    face_indices: &[usize],
    faces: &[MeshFace],
) -> Option<([f64; 3], f64)> {
    let n = vertex_set.len();
    if n < 3 {
        return None;
    }

    // Compute centroid of all vertices
    let mut cx = 0.0_f64;
    let mut cy = 0.0_f64;
    let mut cz = 0.0_f64;
    for &vi in vertex_set {
        let v = &vertices[vi];
        cx += v.x;
        cy += v.y;
        cz += v.z;
    }
    let inv_n = 1.0 / n as f64;
    cx *= inv_n;
    cy *= inv_n;
    cz *= inv_n;

    // Project vertices to (h, r) coordinates
    let mut sum_h = 0.0_f64;
    let mut sum_r = 0.0_f64;
    let mut sum_hh = 0.0_f64;
```

```
    let mut sum_hh = 0.0_f64;
    let mut sum_hr = 0.0_f64;
    let mut h_min = f64::MAX;
    let mut h_max = f64::MIN;

    for &vi in vertex_set {
        let v = &vertices[vi];
        let dx = v.x - cx;
        let dy = v.y - cy;
        let dz = v.z - cz;
        let h = dx * axis_dir[0] + dy * axis_dir[1] + dz * axis_dir[2];
        let rx = dx - h * axis_dir[0];
        let ry = dy - h * axis_dir[1];
        let rz = dz - h * axis_dir[2];
        let r = (rx * rx + ry * ry + rz * rz).sqrt();
        sum_h += h;
        sum_r += r;
        sum_hh += h * h;
        sum_hr += h * r;
        h_min = h_min.min(h);
        h_max = h_max.max(h);
    }

    let nf = n as f64;
    let denom = nf * sum_hh - sum_h * sum_h;

    // Try linear regression first (works when vertices span different heights)
    if denom.abs() > 1e-30 {
        let m = (nf * sum_hr - sum_h * sum_r) / denom;
        let b = (sum_r - m * sum_h) / nf;

        // Reject if nearly cylindrical (|m| < 0.01) or nearly flat (half-angle > 85°)
        if m.abs() >= 0.01 {
            let half_angle = m.abs().atan();
            if half_angle <= 85.0_f64.to_radians() {
                // Apex position: distance from centroid along axis = -b/m
                let apex_t = -b / m;
                let apex = [
                    cx + apex_t * axis_dir[0],
                    cy + apex_t * axis_dir[1],
                    cz + apex_t * axis_dir[2],
                ];
                return Some((apex, half_angle));
            }
        }
    }

    // Fallback: determine half-angle from face normals.
    // For a cone with half-angle θ, the normal at any surface point satisfies:
    //   |n · axis| = sin(θ)
    // We average this over all seed faces (area-weighted) to get a robust estimate.
    let mut weighted_sin = 0.0_f64;
    let mut total_area = 0.0_f64;
    for &fi in face_indices {
        if let Some(normal) = faces[fi].normal {
            let area = face_area(&faces[fi], vertices);
            let sin_theta = dot3(&normal, axis_dir).abs();
            weighted_sin += sin_theta * area;
            total_area += area;
        }
    }
    if total_area < 1e-30 {
        return None;
    }
    let avg_sin = weighted_sin / total_area;
    // sin(θ) must be in (0, 1) for a valid cone (not cylinder or plane)
    if !(0.01..=0.999).contains(&avg_sin) {
        return None;
    }
    let half_angle = avg_sin.asin();

    // Determine apex: for each vertex, the apex is at distance r/tan(θ)
    // below the vertex along the axis. We average over all vertices.
    // Use axis-projected centroid and average radius to find apex.
    let mean_h = sum_h / nf;
    let mean_r = sum_r / nf;
    // On a cone: r = (h - h_apex) * tan(θ), so h_apex = h - r/tan(θ)
    // The sign depends on which side of the apex we're on. Try both orientations.
    let tan_theta = half_angle.tan();
    if tan_theta.abs() < 1e-10 {
        return None;
    }
    let apex_h_candidate = mean_h - mean_r / tan_theta;

    // Apex in world coordinates: centroid + apex_h * axis
    let apex = [
        cx + apex_h_candidate * axis_dir[0],
        cy + apex_h_candidate * axis_dir[1],
        cz + apex_h_candidate * axis_dir[2],
    ];

    Some((apex, half_angle))
}

// ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
// LM refinement for conical fitting
// ---------------------------------------------------------------------------

/// LM problem for cone fitting.
/// Parameters: [alpha, beta, apex_x, apex_y, apex_z, half_angle]
/// alpha, beta: tilt of axis from initial direction in perpendicular directions
struct ConeLMProblem {
    points: Vec<[f64; 3]>,
    a0: [f64; 3],
    u0: [f64; 3],
    w0: [f64; 3],
    params: OVector<f64, Const<6>>,

The previous approach tried multiple strategies and had fallback handling that worked better.

For truncated cones, I'll follow the user's algorithm with line-intersection and eigenvector estimation, though I need to verify this is actually more robust than the regression approach that was working in the integration tests.
> 
> I'm going to implement a hybrid strategy: use the axis-first approach for truncated cones to match the old behavior, and the apex-first approach for pointed cones. This avoids over-engineering with a try-both-paths fallback.

The unit tests all pass now. But the integration test for truncated cones regressed because the new algorithm doesn't work well for truncated cones — the normal-line intersection for apex is unreliable with noisy STL normals. 

The fix: use different estimation strategies for pointed vs truncated cones:
- **Pointed cone** (shared vertex): apex-first, then normal-diff axis
- **Truncated cone** (no shared vertex): axis-first from covariance, then apex from vertex projection

> 💭 I see the issue: when the apex is wrong (e.g., far away from the actual cone tip for a truncated cone), `r/h` ratios are unreliable. For a truncated cone where the apex is computed from the least-squares normal-line intersection, the apex may be inaccurate, leading to bad h values and thus bad half-angle estimates.
> 
> The issue is especially bad when the apex is far from the cone and the intersection point is poorly conditioned for nearby, nearly-parallel normals (which happens with small half-angles).
> 
> For a 9.46° half-angle cone, the normals are nearly parallel. The normal-line intersection becomes ill-conditioned because the lines are nearly parallel. 
> 
> The apex computed from just 3 faces ends up wildly inaccurate. This reveals the core problem: trying to find the apex first via normal-line intersection is fundamentally fragile for truncated cones with small half-angles. The old approach was smarter—it locked down the axis first using normal covariance (which stays robust even with nearly-parallel normals), then derived the apex by projecting vertices along that axis. I should flip the truncated cone logic to do the same: establish the axis first, then compute the apex from vertex projections rather than from intersecting normal lines.
> 
> For each vertex, I'll project it onto the axis to get its height position and radial distance, then fit a line through those points. The x-intercept of that line reveals where the apex sits along the axis. This is the classical cone-fitting approach, and it should be much more stable than what I was doing before.
> 
> Now I'm working through the math more carefully. If I know the axis direction but not the apex location, I can express the cone constraint as a linear relationship: the radial distance equals the height difference from the apex times the tangent of the cone angle. Running linear regression on this gives me both the cone angle and the apex position along the axis simultaneously.
> 
> I still need to figure out how to recover the lateral offset of the apex from the axis. position of A (perpendicular to `a`). For an ideal cone, A lies on the axis, so the lateral position is determined by: for all P_i, (P_i - A - h_i * a) points away from the axis at distance r_i. The axis line passes through A and goes along `a`, so the lateral components are where all the (P_i - h_i * a) converge when r_i → 0.
> 
> Actually, for the initial estimate, a simpler approach:
> 1. Compute axis from normals (already done)
> 2. Compute the "axis line" as the line through the centroid parallel to `a`
> 3. For each vertex, compute h and r relative to this axis line
> 4. Linear regression: r = slope * h + intercept → half_angle = atan(slope), apex offset = -intercept/slope
> 
> Then the apex position is the centroid shifted along the axis direction by the apex offset, adjusted for the lateral position of the axis line. To find this axis line more directly, I can project all vertices onto the plane perpendicular to `a` and average those projections to get the lateral center, then project vertices along `a` to get their heights, compute distances from the axis line, and use linear regression on r versus h to extract the cone's half-angle and apex location.
> 
> I think the more robust approach is to stick with the normal-line intersection for the apex estimate, but add a fallback that computes the half-angle from the distribution of normals themselves. For a cone with half-angle θ, all surface normals make an angle of (π/2 - θ) with the axis, so I can compute θ = π/2 - median(acos(|n·a|)). This decouples the half-angle estimate from the apex, so a poor apex doesn't throw off the angle. Then Levenberg-Marquardt can refine both starting from the axis direction, which is already stable.
> 
> The real bottleneck is the LM convergence—the residuals aren't decreasing as expected from the initial guess.
> 
> Looking at the actual numbers, the half-angle estimate is coming out to 0.2274 radians (13.0°) when it should be around 0.1651 radians (9.46°), which is a significant error. The test pipeline expects exactly one hypothesis but gets zero because the BFS expansion finds seeds and grows to 4 faces, but then the acceptance criteria rejects all of them. I need to trace through what those acceptance thresholds are actually checking.
> 
> The BFS metrics show 45 total faces visited across all runs with a maximum expansion of 4 faces per run, so the seed fit is producing a poor initial cone that severely limits how far the expansion can reach. /// Given an axis direction and a set of vertices, project them to (h, r) coordinates
/// relative to the centroid, then fit a linear profile r = m·h + b.
/// Returns (apex, half_angle) or None if degenerate.
///
/// The cone axis goes through the centroid. We compute:
///   h_i = (v_i - centroid) · â
///   r_i = |(v_i - centroid) - h_i·â|
/// Then linear regression r = m·h + b gives:
///   half_angle = atan(|m|)
///   apex = centroid - (b/m)·â  (if m != 0)
///
/// If the linear regression is degenerate (e.g., all vertices at same height),
/// we fall back to a normal-based approach using face normals to determine the
/// half-angle and vertex positions to locate the apex.
fn fit_cone_profile(
    axis_dir: &[f64; 3],
    vertex_set: &HashSet<usize>,
    vertices: &[MeshVertex],
    face_indices: &[usize],
    faces: &[MeshFace],
) -> Option<([f64; 3], f64)> {
    let n = vertex_set.len();
    if n < 3 {
        return None;
    }

    // Compute centroid of all vertices
    let mut cx = 0.0_f64;
    let mut cy = 0.0_f64;
    let mut cz = 0.0_f64;
    for &vi in vertex_set {
        let v = &vertices[vi];
        cx += v.x;
        cy += v.y;
        cz += v.z;
    }
    let inv_n = 1.0 / n as f64;
    cx *= inv_n;
    cy *= inv_n;
    cz *= inv_n;

    // Project vertices to (h, r) coordinates
    let mut sum_h = 0.0_f64;
    let mut sum_r = 0.0_f64;
    let mut sum_hh = 0.0_f64;
    let mut sum_hr = 0.0_f64;
    let mut h_min = f64::MAX;
    let mut h_max = f64::MIN;

    for &vi in vertex_set {
        let v = &vertices[vi];
        let dx = v.x - cx;
        let dy = v.y - cy;
        let dz = v.z - cz;
        let h = dx * axis_dir[0] + dy * axis_dir[1] + dz * axis_dir[2];
        let rx = dx - h * axis_dir[0];
        let ry = dy - h * axis_dir[1];
        let rz = dz - h * axis_dir[2];
        let r = (rx * rx + ry * ry + rz * rz).sqrt();
        sum_h += h;
        sum_r += r;
        sum_hh += h * h;
        sum_hr += h * r;
        h_min = h_min.min(h);
        h_max = h_max.max(h);
    }

    let nf = n as f64;
    let denom = nf * sum_hh - sum_h * sum_h;

    // Try linear regression first (works when vertices span different heights)
    if denom.abs() > 1e-30 {
        let m = (nf * sum_hr - sum_h * sum_r) / denom;
        let b = (sum_r - m * sum_h) / nf;

        // Reject if nearly cylindrical (|m| < 0.01) or nearly flat (half-angle > 85°)
        if m.abs() >= 0.01 {
            let half_angle = m.abs().atan();
            if half_angle <= 85.0_f64.to_radians() {
                // Apex position: distance from centroid along axis = -b/m
                let apex_t = -b / m;
                let apex = [
                    cx + apex_t * axis_dir[0],
                    cy + apex_t * axis_dir[1],
                    cz + apex_t * axis_dir[2],
                ];
                return Some((apex, half_angle));

/// Given an axis direction and a set of vertices, project them to (h, r) coordinates
/// relative to the centroid, then fit a linear profile r = m·h + b.
/// Returns (apex, half_angle) or None if degenerate.
///
/// The cone axis goes through the centroid. We compute:
///   h_i = (v_i - centroid) · â
///   r_i = |(v_i - centroid) - h_i·â|
/// Then linear regression r = m·h + b gives:
///   half_angle = atan(|m|)
///   apex = centroid - (b/m)·â  (if m != 0)
///
/// If the linear regression is degenerate (e.g., all vertices at same height),
/// we fall back to a normal-based approach using face normals to determine the
/// half-angle and vertex positions to locate the apex.
fn fit_cone_profile(
    axis_dir: &[f64; 3],
    vertex_set: &HashSet<usize>,
    vertices: &[MeshVertex],
    face_indices: &[usize],
    faces: &[MeshFace],
) -> Option<([f64; 3], f64)> {
    let n = vertex_set.len();
    if n < 3 {
        return None;
    }

    // Compute centroid of all vertices
    let mut cx = 0.0_f64;
    let mut cy = 0.0_f64;
    let mut cz = 0.0_f64;
    for &vi in vertex_set {
        let v = &vertices[vi];
        cx += v.x;
        cy += v.y;
        cz += v.z;
    }
    let inv_n = 1.0 / n as f64;
    cx *= inv_n;
    cy *= inv_n;
    cz *= inv_n;

    // Project vertices to (h, r) coordinates
    let mut sum_h = 0.0_f64;
    let mut sum_r = 0.0_f64;
    let mut sum_hh = 0.0_f64;
    let mut sum_hr = 0.0_f64;
    let mut h_min = f64::MAX;
    let mut h_max = f64::MIN;

    for &vi in vertex_set {
        let v = &vertices[vi];
        let dx = v.x - cx;
        let dy = v.y - cy;
        let dz = v.z - cz;
        let h = dx * axis_dir[0] + dy * axis_dir[1] + dz * axis_dir[2];
        let rx = dx - h * axis_dir[0];
        let ry = dy - h * axis_dir[1];
        let rz = dz - h * axis_dir[2];
        let r = (rx * rx + ry * ry + rz * rz).sqrt();
        sum_h += h;
        sum_r += r;
        sum_hh += h * h;
        sum_hr += h * r;
        h_min = h_min.min(h);
        h_max = h_max.max(h);
    }

    let nf = n as f64;
    let denom = nf * sum_hh - sum_h * sum_h;

    // Try linear regression first (works when vertices span different heights)
    if denom.abs() > 1e-30 {
        let m = (nf * sum_hr - sum_h * sum_r) / denom;
        let b = (sum_r - m * sum_h) / nf;

        // Reject if nearly cylindrical (|m| < 0.01) or nearly flat (half-angle > 85°)
        if m.abs() >= 0.01 {
            let half_angle = m.abs().atan();
            if half_angle <= 85.0_f64.to_radians() {
                // Apex position: distance from centroid along axis = -b/m
                let apex_t = -b / m;
                let apex = [
                    cx + apex_t * axis_dir[0],
                    cy + apex_t * axis_dir[1],
                    cz + apex_t * axis_dir[2],
                ];
                return Some((apex, half_angle));
            }
        }
    }

    // Fallback: determine half-angle from face normals.
    // For a cone with half-angle θ, the normal at any surface point satisfies:
    //   |n · axis| = sin(θ)
    // We average this over all seed faces (area-weighted) to get a robust estimate.
    let mut weighted_sin = 0.0_f64;
    let mut total_area = 0.0_f64;
    for &fi in face_indices {
        if let Some(normal) = faces[fi].normal {
            let area = face_area(&faces[fi], vertices);
            let sin_theta = dot3(&normal, axis_dir).abs();
            weighted_sin += sin_theta * area;
            total_area += area;
        }
    }
    if total_area < 1e-30 {
        return None;
    }
    let avg_sin = weighted_sin / total_area;
    // sin(θ) must be in (0, 1) for a valid cone (not cylinder or plane)
    if !(0.01..=0.999).contains(&avg_sin) {
        return None;
    }
    let half_angle = avg_sin.asin();

    // Determine ap

fn fit_cone(
    face_indices: &[usize],
    vertex_set: &HashSet<usize>,
    faces: &[MeshFace],
    vertices: &[MeshVertex],
) -> Option<([f64; 3], [f64; 3], f64)> {
    if vertex_set.len() < 4 {
        return None;
    }

    // Determine initial axis direction using multiple strategies.
    let mut axis_dir = compute_cone_axis(face_indices, vertex_set, faces, vertices)?;

    // Fit cone profile to get initial apex and half-angle.
    let (mut apex, half_angle) = fit_cone_profile(&axis_dir, vertex_set, vertices, face_indices, faces)?;

    // Ensure axis points from apex toward the bulk of vertices (positive h side).
    // vertex_to_cone_distance assumes h = (v - apex) · axis > 0 for points on the cone.
    let mut h_sum = 0.0_f64;
    for &vi in vertex_set.iter() {
        let v = &vertices[vi];
        h_sum += (v.x - apex[0]) * axis_dir[0]
               + (v.y - apex[1]) * axis_dir[1]
               + (v.z - apex[2]) * axis_dir[2];
    }
    if h_sum < 0.0 {
        axis_dir = [-axis_dir[0], -axis_dir[1], -axis_dir[2]];
        // Re-fit profile with corrected axis (apex position may adjust)
        if let Some((new_apex, new_ha)) = fit_cone_profile(&axis_dir, vertex_set, vertices, face_indices, faces) {
            apex = new_apex;
            // half_angle should be the same
            let _ = new_ha;
        }
    }

    // Only use LM refinement when we have enough vertices for the 6-param problem.
    // With < 10 vertices, the LM is underconstrained and produces bad results.
    if vertex_set.len() >= 10 {
        let points: Vec<[f64; 3]> = vertex_set.iter()
            .map(|&vi| [vertices[vi].x, vertices[vi].y, vertices[vi].z])
            .collect();
        let problem = ConeLMProblem::new(points, axis_dir, apex, half_angle);
        let (result, _report) = LevenbergMarquardt::new().minimize(problem);
        let refined_dir = result.axis_dir_from_params(&result.params);
        let refined_apex = [result.params[2], result.params[3], result.params[4]];
        let refined_half_angle = result.params[5];

        if refined_half_angle > 0.005 && refined_half_angle < 85.0_f64.to_radians() {
            return Some((refined_apex, refined_dir, refined_half_angle));
        }
    }

    Some((apex, axis_dir, half_angle))
}

        d[2] - t * axis_direction[2],

> I swapped out `compute_cone_axis` for `compute_truncated_cone_axis`, but looking at the implementations, the axis computation should be identical — both use normal covariance for ≥8 faces and fall back to the shared vertex approach for fewer faces. > 3. n_faces=14, half_angle=0.00° (cylinder) → var ≈ 0.000855
> 4. n_faces=65, half_angle=16.87° (cone) → var ≈ 0.000445
> 5. n_faces=18, half_angle=0.00° (cylinder) → var ≈ 0.000666
>

> 
> The pattern is becoming clearer: I need to tighten the half_angle threshold to reject near-planar cones above 80°, but the cylinder false positives are trickier since a cylinder along a great circle naturally produces near-zero normal variance, making it hard to distinguish from actual cylinders using normals alone.
> 
> For case #4 with 65 faces and var=0.000445, this is the real challenge — it's a false cone detection on a sphere where the axis aligns with the pole, creating a narrow latitude band with remarkably consistent normals. The math checks out: if the cone axis passes through the sphere center, the dot product n·axis = cos(θ) for faces at colatitude θ, so a tight band around any latitude produces tiny variance even though it's genuinely part of a sphere.
> 
> Looking at tightening the variance threshold from 0.001 to 0.0005 would catch cases #3, #5, and #4, but my real cone data shows even tighter variance values around 0.000038-0.000039 with 35-203 faces, so this approach risks false negatives on actual cones.
> 
> I'm considering a two-pronged strategy instead: reject half-angles above 80° to eliminate the extreme cases, and simultaneously tighten the normal variance threshold to 0.0005. I should also verify this against my real cone data at the refit level to see how it performs.

124:For now, each face can belong to a single hypothesis of each type. Hopefully that's sufficient since hypotheses should nearly exactly match the vertices, but it's possible that in the future we may need to keep a set of candidate hypothesis indices per type, then select the best one in stage 2.6.
179:Stages 2.1–2.5 share a common algorithm structure for deducing surface hypotheses from the mesh. This section describes the generic algorithm; each stage section below describes how it is parameterized. The Rust implementation uses the `SurfaceType` trait in `bfs.rs` to share code across surface types.
248:**Acceptance tests**: `face_count >= 2`. Single-face planes are not kept as hypotheses. Faces that end up in a single-face group are left as `NO_HYPOTHESIS`; stage 2.6 synthesizes single-face planar hypotheses for any faces that received no hypothesis from stages 2.1–2.5.
280:Fit spherical hypotheses to connected sets of faces that lie on a common sphere. Faces with conical/cylindrical hypotheses are not excluded — a face can legitimately belong to both a cylinder and a sphere (e.g., equator faces). Stage 2.6 resolves overlapping hypotheses later. A spherical hypothesis consists of:
324:#### 2.5 Select surfaces to use for reconstruction
341:4. Assign any remaining unassigned faces to their single-face planar hypothesis (fallback for faces on surfaces not yet fitted: ruled, freeform). Stage 2.7 may replace some of these with NURBS surfaces.
356:- `onshape_pipe_elbow_stage26_compare`: Passes `--compare` with 290 cylindrical + 0 toroidal selections. The pipe elbow's torus surface is tessellated into many narrow strips whose normals span < 180°, so stage 2.5 torus fitting doesn't fire (normals look locally cylindrical). This is expected — the torus is decomposed into cylindrical patches that individually pass `--compare`.
363:#### 2.6 Surface refitting (optional)
365:After stage 2.6 selects surfaces, some border faces may have been absorbed by a hypothesis that fits within tolerance but isn't the best fit. This step refines assignments and re-fits surfaces to improve accuracy.
370:2. For each such face, compute the vertex-to-surface distance for each candidate surface (using the selected surfaces from 2.6, not the raw hypotheses).
374:This step is expected to be most useful at boundaries between surfaces of different types (e.g., where a cylinder meets a plane), where BFS may have greedily absorbed a border face that technically fits within tolerance but belongs more naturally to the adjacent surface. It may be unnecessary if the greedy area-based selection in 2.6 already produces clean boundaries — implement only if stage 3 reconstruction reveals boundary accuracy problems.
378:For groups of adjacent faces that remain as single-face planar hypotheses after stages 2.1–2.5 (i.e., they don't fit any analytic surface), try to fit a NURBS or B-spline surface.
406:- `selected_surface_idx: usize` — Index into the selected surfaces from stage

## Interactive viz features
Added to `--viz-stages` window: initial zoom with 15% margin, help overlay (`?`), face number labels (`N`) with depth-buffer occlusion testing, hover face info (index/area/normal via ray-cast picking), and click-to-skip-seeding (left-click sends `SkipToSeed(face)` to BFS). Integrated egui for all text overlays. Restructured render loop so egui runs after 3D rendering to enable depth-buffer readback.

## Viz bug fixes: occlusion, hover highlight, click-to-skip deadlock
- Replaced unreliable depth-buffer-based face label occlusion with face-normal culling: labels for back-facing faces (normal pointing away from camera) are hidden.
- Added thick bright-yellow wireframe highlight on the hovered face; hovered face's number is drawn larger with a white halo for readability.
- Fixed click-to-skip deadlock: click only sends `SkipToSeed` when `waiting_for_input = true` (BFS is blocked in `recv`), and immediately sets `waiting_for_input = false` so the render loop calls `try_recv()` again and can receive the next overlay — without this the sync channel would deadlock.

## Delete legacy per-type trial BFS functions
Removed 1,305 lines of dead code left over from the pre-generic BFS implementation: `run_cone_trial_bfs()` and `ConeTrialResult` from conical.rs, `run_torus_trial_bfs()` and `TorusTrialResult` from toroidal.rs, `all_vertices_within_*_tolerance()` helpers from conical/toroidal/spherical/planar, `build_vertex_to_faces_map()` and `SphericalSurface::vertex_to_faces()` from spherical.rs, `vertex_to_plane_distance()` from planar.rs, and `viz_custom()` from mod.rs. Cleaned up all `#[allow(dead_code)]` annotations in stage2 and removed unused imports (`VecDeque`, `VizAction`, `VizSender`, `viz_custom`, `viz_face_centroid`, `viz_face_normal`, `REFIT_SKIP_MULTIPLIER`, `build_normal_covariance`) from conical.rs and toroidal.rs.
```

# Brepper - STL to STEP Converter

Convert triangulated STL meshes from CAD exports into parametric STEP files with fitted analytic and freeform surfaces.

## Usage

```
brepper - Convert STL mesh to STEP with fitted surfaces

USAGE:
    brepper [OPTIONS] <input.stl> [-o <output.step>]

REQUIRED:
    <input.stl>              Input STL file (binary or ASCII)

GENERAL OPTIONS:
    -o <step>, --output=<step>  Output STEP file
    --stl-units=<units>      Units used by the STL file (mm, cm, m, in, ft, um; default: mm)
    --step-units=<units>     Units to use in exported STEP file (default: mm)
    --compare=<step>         STEP file to compare to at each step
    --vertex-tolerance=<value>  Fitting tolerance in STL units (default: 1e-5)
    --surface-tolerance=<value> Surface-to-face offset tolerance in STL units (default: 0.4)
    --angular-tolerance=<deg>   Max dihedral angle between adjacent triangles on the same surface (default: 17.5)
    -v, --verbose[=LEVEL]    Verbosity level: 1 (or bare -v) = summaries, -vv or --verbose=2 = per-face details, -vvv or --verbose=3 = full BFS trace
    -q, --quiet              Suppress non-error output
    --debug                  Enable debug output and intermediate files
    --stage=<stage>          Stop after stage, e.g. 2.2 (default: 4.1). --stage=2 stops after all of stage2
    --viz-stages=<stages>    Open interactive 3D visualization at specified stages (e.g. 2.1,2.2,2.3)
```

### Examples

```bash
# Full conversion
./target/debug/brepper input.stl -o output.step

# Just load and validate mesh (stage 1.2)
./target/debug/brepper input.stl --stage 1.2 -v

# Compare mesh against reference STEP at each stage
./target/debug/brepper input.stl --compare reference.step --stage 1 -v
```

See [DEVELOPMENT_PLAN.md](DEVELOPMENT_PLAN.md) for full documentation.

### Tolerance Settings

#### Surface Tolerance

This is how far the surface of a mesh triangle is allowed to deviate from the surface it is approximating.
````

124:For now, each face can belong to a single hypothesis of each type. Hopefully that's sufficient since hypotheses should nearly exactly match the vertices, but it's possible that in the future we may need to keep a set of candidate hypothesis indices per type, then select the best one in stage 2.6.
179:Stages 2.1–2.5 share a common algorithm structure for deducing surface hypotheses from the mesh. This section describes the generic algorithm; each stage section below describes how it is parameterized. The Rust implementation uses the `SurfaceType` trait in `bfs.rs` to share code across surface types.
248:**Acceptance tests**: `face_count >= 2`. Single-face planes are not kept as hypotheses. Faces that end up in a single-face group are left as `NO_HYPOTHESIS`; stage 2.6 synthesizes single-face planar hypotheses for any faces that received no hypothesis from stages 2.1–2.5.
280:Fit spherical hypotheses to connected sets of faces that lie on a common sphere. Faces with conical/cylindrical hypotheses are not excluded — a face can legitimately belong to both a cylinder and a sphere (e.g., equator faces). Stage 2.6 resolves overlapping hypotheses later. A spherical hypothesis consists of:
324:#### 2.5 Select surfaces to use for reconstruction
341:4. Assign any remaining unassigned faces to their single-face planar hypothesis (fallback for faces on surfaces not yet fitted: ruled, freeform). Stage 2.7 may replace some of these with NURBS surfaces.
349:6. **Prune unselected hypotheses**: remove any hypothesis that was not selected from the hypothesis vectors in `Stage2Output`. Remap `SelectedSurface` indices to match the compacted vectors. After this step, `Stage2Output.conical_hypotheses` (etc.) contain only selected hypotheses.
357:- `onshape_pipe_elbow_stage26_compare`: Passes `--compare` with 290 cylindrical + 0 toroidal selections. The pipe elbow's torus surface is tessellated into many narrow strips whose normals span < 180°, so stage 2.5 torus fitting doesn't fire (normals look locally cylindrical). This is expected — the torus is decomposed into cylindrical patches that individually pass `--compare`.
364:#### 2.6 Surface refitting (optional)
366:After stage 2.6 selects surfaces, some border faces may have been absorbed by a hypothesis that fits within tolerance but isn't the best fit. This step refines assignments and re-fits surfaces to improve accuracy.
371:2. For each such face, compute the vertex-to-surface distance for each candidate surface (using the selected surfaces from 2.6, not the raw hypotheses).
375:This step is expected to be most useful at boundaries between surfaces of different types (e.g., where a cylinder meets a plane), where BFS may have greedily absorbed a border face that technically fits within tolerance but belongs more naturally to the adjacent surface. It may be unnecessary if the greedy area-based selection in 2.6 already produces clean boundaries — implement only if stage 3 reconstruction reveals boundary
... (truncated, 3366 chars total)

**Step 7: Add new learnings.** From this session, two key insights worth recording:

1. Pointed cone LM refinement needs a fixed-apex formulation (the general 7-parameter LM is underdetermined when all faces share the apex).
2. Post-selection pruning of unselected hypotheses simplifies downstream code and makes hypothesis indices match selected surfaces.

Robust cone fitting for pointed cones + prune unselected hypotheses

Cone fitting:
- Rewrote cone fitting algorithm to handle both pointed and truncated cones
- Pointed cones (all faces share apex vertex): apex-vertex seeding with
  fixed-apex 3-parameter LM refinement (axis direction + half-angle only)
- Truncated cones: multi-strategy axis estimation from normal differences
  with full 7-parameter LM refinement
- Added acceptance test with normal-axis variance threshold (<angular_tol/2)
  and half-angle bounds (2°-85°) to prevent false positive cones on
  spherical/cylindrical patches
- Added pointed-cone unit test (10 triangular faces, apex at tip)

Surface selection:
- Implemented post-selection pruning of unselected hypotheses via
  prune_unselected_hypotheses() in stage2/mod.rs
- After select_surfaces assigns faces, removes any hypothesis not selected
  from planes/cylinders/spheres/cones/tori vectors
- Remaps SelectedSurface indices to match compacted hypothesis vectors

```
135:- Available primitives: `box()`, `cylinder(diameter, height)`, `sphere(diameter)`, `cone(d1, d2, height)`, `wedge()`, `hex_prism()`.
140:- ccad `profile_xz` only supports polylines (no arcs), so `revolve()` creates conical strip approximations, NOT true TOROIDAL_SURFACE. To create true toroidal surfaces in STEP via ccad, use fillet operations on circular edges (cylinder-plane junctions).
143:- Cone-sphere tangency construction: for a cone with half-angle α (from axis). At a tangent circle of radius r on the cone, the tangent sphere has radius R = r / cos(α), and its center sits on the cone axis at distance r·tan(α) beyond the tangent circle in the apex direction. Using `union(cone, sphere)` in ccad produces an exact tangent junction in the STEP output.
162:- Minimum 3-face requirement: cylinder hypotheses must contain at least 3 faces. Any real cylindrical surface from a CAD tessellation will have at least 3 facets around its circumference. This eliminates spurious 2-face cylinder fits from locally adjacent faces on tori, cones, and spheres (e.g., cone went from 29 to 0 spurious fits, pipe_elbow from 252 to 51).
239:- **Plane-cone and cylinder-cone junctions are never tangent**: At a plane-cone junction (plane ⊥ axis at base), the normals differ by the cone half-angle. At a cylinder-cone junction (coaxial), the cone normal has an axial tilt of sin(half_angle) that the cylinder normal lacks. For any real cone (half-angle > 2°), these exceed the tangency threshold. No test model has cone tangent edges; all cone models pass via the non-tangent IntSS path.
329:### Conical surface detection: apex-vertex seeding
331:Triple-seed (3 adjacent faces) fails for cones because apex-adjacent faces share the apex vertex and only span ~12° of the cone base — this gives a biased centroid, tilted axis, and wrong half-angle (~2° instead of true ~20°). Instead, use **apex-vertex seeding**: identify vertices with many incident non-coplanar-normal faces (eigenval/trace ratio < 0.3 from normal covariance), then use ALL faces incident to each apex vertex as the seed set. This gives robust axis estimation.
333:### Cone axis orientation must be explicit
335:The normal covariance eigenvector gives an unsigned axis direction. The cone distance formula `d = r·cos(θ) - h·sin(θ)` requires h > 0 (vertices on the apex-to-base side). If the axis happens to point the wrong way, h is negative for base vertices and the distance formula produces huge false errors (~14mm instead of ~0). Fix: after profile fitting, check if the sum of all h values is negative and flip the axis if so, then re-fit the profile.
337:### Conical faces need `is_periodic` treatment
339:In stage 3, conical faces are periodic (full-revolution) like cylindrical and spherical faces. The `is_periodic` check must include `SelectedSurface::Conical(_)` or conical faces will go through `create_planar_face` instead of `create_periodic_face`, causing segfaults when a full-circle edge is encountered.

343:`geom::ConicalSurface::new_ax3_real2(&ax3, half_angle, ref_radius)` — for brepper's cones, set origin at the apex and ref_radius=0. The Ax3 origin is the apex point, Z-direction points from apex toward the base.
345:### False positive cone detection on spherical/cylindrical patches
347:Sphere and cylinder patches can be approximately fitted as cones because locally any smooth surface looks conical. Key discriminators:
350:3. **Normal-axis consistency**: On a true cone, every face normal makes the same angle with the axis (= 90° - half_angle). Compute std-dev of `acos(|n·axis|)` across all faces — if it exceeds `angular_tol / 2`, the surface isn't a cone (likely a sphere).
